ifdef::lang-en[]
= Take Documents Back — Manuscripts in AsciiDoc, Forms in aiseed office

*What you take back in documents is not the storage. It is how you hold the manuscript.*

Inside the gate (2-05), the first tool to place is documents. But the conclusion
of this chapter is not "which editor to stand up." As of 2026, a document has
three places it can live: Office, your own stack, and Claude Docs. All three are
convenient, and in all three the key leans toward either someone else or you. So
the thing to decide first is *what form the manuscript is held in.*

== Office is something you pass through, not something you work inside

Do not misread the reason for stepping away from Office. This is not about
efficiency. Thirty minutes becoming thirty seconds happens as a side effect; it
is not the point.

Split clerical work into three.

* *The entrance* — files that arrive from other people (Word, Excel, PDF)
* *The middle* — where you think, work, and save
* *The exit* — files you hand to other people (Word, Excel, PDF)

Most people have done all three inside Office. A Word file arrives, you open it
in Word, edit it as Word, and send it back as Word. But as long as the middle is
also Office, AI never becomes a colleague — the next section shows why.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  In["Entrance<br/>Word / Excel / PDF<br/>(from others)"]
  Mid["Middle<br/>AsciiDoc manuscript + structured data<br/>(in git)"]
  Out["Exit<br/>Word / PDF / HTML<br/>(to others)"]
  Claude(("AI"))

  In -->|pass through| Mid
  Mid <-->|discuss, decide| Claude
  Mid -->|pass through| Out

  classDef office fill:#fef3e7,stroke:#c89559,color:#5a3f1a
  classDef native fill:#e8f5e9,stroke:#7a9a6d,color:#3a4d34
  class In,Out office
  class Mid native
```

So treat `.docx`, `.xlsx`, and `.pptx` not as where the content lives, but as
exchange formats for dealing with people at the entrance and the exit. Do not
change the organization's rules. Take back control of your own middle, and
nothing else.

== Inside Office, AI never becomes a colleague

Why move the content out of a closed format? Because *as long as you are inside
Office, AI stays a tool and never becomes a colleague.*

Hand a Word file to AI and a conversion happens every time. Unzip the `.docx`,
read the XML, strip the formatting, pull out the text. Excel is the same: cell
coordinates, formatting, merged cells, and cross-sheet references sit between the
AI and the content.

The result is that AI is usable but not a colleague. Ask it to read the whole
thing and organize the issues, and the layout breaks. Ask it to analyze a table,
and merged cells and formatted values confuse it.

The moment the content drops down to text and structured data, that wall
disappears. The AI reads directly, writes directly, and gives back judgment.
Office survives as an editing tool. The content, though, stays somewhere the AI
can touch.

== From the person who processes to the person who decides

As long as you do clerical work inside Office, you remain the person who
processes: the one who totals the spreadsheet, tidies the document, re-pastes the
numbers. That work can be replaced by AI, and as AI gets cheaper, organizations
pull people out of the role.

Bring the content down to structure and the role changes. You become the person
who works out what should be done, decides how to read it, designs the new
mechanism, and decides what to ask the AI for. Not the amount of work but the
substance of the work changes.

____
AI replaces the person who processes. It cannot replace the person who decides.
Taking back the content means moving to the side that is not replaced.
____

=== A worked example: the monthly report — same report, different job

Take the monthly sales report.

*The old flow.* Open the sales data in Excel. Pivot it. Build the chart. Paste it
into Word. Write the prose. Export a PDF and send it to your manager. The job you
are doing here is tidying numbers.

*The new flow.* Read the underlying data; an arriving `.xlsx` is ingested by
machine. Have the AI write the aggregation and produce a table. Embed the chart.
Let the AI draft the prose. *You add the interpretation and the judgment.* Convert
to `.docx` or PDF at the exit. The job you are doing here is working out what the
numbers mean.

"This month is up 12% on last month — which customer drove it? Does it continue?
Should the sales strategy change?" Those questions never surfaced while you were
running pivot operations. With structured data in hand and an AI beside you, they
come up on their own.

== Hold the manuscript as text, and commit only the manuscript

This is the center of the chapter. There are only two decisions.

*First, hold the manuscript as AsciiDoc (`.adoc`) text.* It is a text file, so git
gives you a diff. Who changed which line, and when, is on the record. A `.docx`
is compressed XML, so its diff is unreadable. Held as text, the AI reads it and
writes it directly.

*Second, commit only the manuscript; keep the design out of git.* The template
that decides the appearance lives somewhere other than the repository. Apply a
different template to the same manuscript and it comes out in a different form.
What remains in git history is only the diff of what was said.

```
manuscripts/      ← in git. only .adoc text
  2026-09-report.adoc
templates/        ← not in git. appearance
  report.tmpl.adoc
output/           ← not in git. what was printed
  2026-09-report.pdf
```

Everything downstream gets easier. If the manuscript is text, you choose the
exit: print a PDF, export a `.docx` for someone else, render HTML for the site.
All of them come out of the same manuscript.

And *exchanging data as AsciiDoc is going to matter.* Between people, and between
people and AI, once the thing being exchanged is text, nothing is lost in
transit.

== Form-fidelity work belongs to aiseed office — ONLYOFFICE does not hold Japanese forms

Manuscripts can be text. Forms cannot. Take a `.docx` application form, open it
at the prescribed page count, with the prescribed line positions and column
widths, correct it, and print it. In that job, format compatibility *is* the
content.

For that job, *ONLYOFFICE does not hold up on Japanese forms.* That is why
aiseed office (https://github.com/aiseed-dev/officework[`aiseed-dev/officework`])
is being built: an engine that reads and writes `.docx` and `.xlsx` and typesets
straight to PDF.

```bash
pip install officework
```

As checked on 2026-09-20, the engine is at *0.5.0* on PyPI, published 2026-09-04.
The beta label is off. The published release notes carry a line-by-line
comparison against PDFs that Japanese government offices publish.

|===
| Document | Mean line-end deviation | Lines within 1pt
| Survey form (110 lines) | 9.01pt → 2.50pt | 5 → 68
| Notice (141 lines) | 3.71pt → 2.54pt | 5 → 83
|===

Write down what has not arrived, too. *Only the engine is distributed; the GUI
application is not yet.* Some documents wrap one character off because of how
table column widths are taken. Some spreadsheets paginate differently from the
original. And *reading and writing AsciiDoc is not shipped yet.* The decision in
the previous section is a direction; this tool does not do all of it today.

== A document now has three places to live — where to put Claude Docs

On 2026-09-16, Claude Docs shipped in beta. Creating a document in conversation,
editing it directly, commenting on it together, and sharing it all happen inside
Claude. As checked on 2026-09-20 it is available on Pro, Max, Team, and
Enterprise.

It is convenient. Nothing to install. As a place to draft, revise, and talk
something through, it takes fewer steps than anything else available.

At the same time, the structure is the same as Microsoft 365's.

* The document itself lives inside claude.ai. *You cannot go and fetch it from
  outside.* Only Claude's own tools can read it
* Opening a shared link requires the other person to have a Claude account
* Team and Enterprise documents cannot yet be shared outside the organization
* Organizations using customer-managed encryption keys, zero data retention, or a
  HIPAA-ready configuration cannot use it yet

One thing is different. *There is a clear exit.* It exports to Word, PDF,
Markdown, and Google Docs. Exporting as far as Markdown means the manuscript can
come down to your own disk as text.

So its place is settled. *Use Claude Docs as the place to draft and circulate a
manuscript. Export the finished manuscript as Markdown, convert it to AsciiDoc,
and commit it.* The decision in the previous section then holds: the content stays
with you, and Claude Docs becomes a door you pass through.

Settle how to compare the cost here, too. The comparison is not against a free
tool. It is against *what you already pay a vendor.* If a production company
writes the copy for your public site, compare against that invoice. If you pay
per-seat licenses every month, compare against that total. As of 2026-09-20,
Claude Pro is $20 a month.

____
A document now has three places to live. The question is not which to pick.
Hold the manuscript as text and all three become doors you pass through.
____

== Why not add another storage application

"Documents" tempts you to stand up a whole OneDrive replacement like Nextcloud.
But Nextcloud is a heavy PHP monolith that drags in its own user system and its
own database. It points the opposite way from the single-binary lightness this
part has chosen throughout.

In 2-05 you already stood up PocketBase, which holds authentication. For document
storage, make that the gate and files are enough for the rest.

* *The thing itself* — the file. No heavy storage application
* *Authentication* — 2-05's gate works as-is. Do not create a second login
* *Permissions and sharing* — held by a local structured store, with the gate confirming identity

== The files stay files — the gate holds identity, a store holds permissions

Keep documents as plain files on your own storage. Do not embed them as blobs in
records. Keep `.adoc`, `.docx`, and `.xlsx` as files, because then machines
(Polars, AI) can read them directly later.

The first choice is where permissions live.

* *A structured store (recommended)* — if PostgreSQL is already up, put access
  control there. For a small setup with only the gate, the gate's SQLite is
  enough. It can be queried, transactions apply, and `pg_dump` preserves it
* *xattr (minimum dependencies)* — if you refuse to add any store, pin them to the
  file's extended attributes. But xattrs are silently dropped by `cp`, `rsync`,
  `tar`, and backups. Confirm that attributes survive every copy and backup path

```sql
-- access control in a structured store: queryable, and it does not vanish
-- doc_acl(doc_id, subject, perm); subject is 'alice' or 'team:sales'
SELECT doc_id FROM doc_acl WHERE subject IN ('alice', 'team:sales') AND perm >= 'r';
```

You do not need a heavy separate application just for permissions. The gate holds
identity; the structured store holds permissions.

== Moving existing documents

Documents piled up in OneDrive and SharePoint move without a format change.

```bash
# pull them out with rclone and lay them on your own storage
rclone copy onedrive:Documents ./inbox --progress
# record each file's permissions into the structured store (one script run)
```

Migrate in stages. Run both sides in parallel, and cancel the old storage only
after the move is finished (2-12).

Write everything new as `.adoc` from the start. You do not have to convert the
back catalog. *Start with new manuscripts as text, and the storage turns over on
its own.*

== Dealing with the outside — hand it over at the exit

You do not need a dedicated sharing platform for customers and suppliers. What
goes outside goes out through the exit: not the source file, but a `.docx` or a
PDF, sent by mail (2-08). Receiving is the same — a file that arrives is opened at
the entrance and then brought in.

Do not create gate accounts for outsiders. Parties who genuinely need to co-edit
are rare; exchanging versions is almost always enough. Send it, it comes back
corrected. The way it has worked since paper.

== People use the editor, machines use Polars

This is where 2-03 pays off. *People enter and read through an editing tool;
machines handle the underlying data with Polars and DuckDB.* The same `.xlsx` is
an editing tool to a person and an input to a machine.

* Summary sheets, application forms, proposals a person writes — opened and stored in aiseed office
* Millions of rows reconciled, company-wide totals — Polars reads the `.xlsx` and writes PostgreSQL

The spreadsheet goes back to being a human tool, and the heavy processing moves
to the machine's tools.

== A reference implementation — kura

This arrangement is actually built in the public repository *kura*
(https://github.com/aiseed-dev/workspace[`aiseed-dev/workspace`]), a self-hosted
alternative to Microsoft 365 and Google Workspace aimed at small and mid-sized
business use.

* *Authentication* — PocketBase (replaceable token verification with a short cache)
* *Permissions* — minimum dependencies, held in the file's xattrs
* *The thing itself* — the file
* *Editing* — an editing engine layered on with JWT and a callback (FastAPI + Flet)

== How to check you are done

This chapter is done when these five hold.

. Your own `.docx` form opens in aiseed office and prints at the same page count as Word
. An `.adoc` manuscript committed to git shows only the lines you changed in the diff
. Both a PDF and a `.docx` come out of the same manuscript
. A person logged in through the gate sees only the documents they have rights to
. Copies made with `cp -a` and `rsync -X` still carry the permissions (if you use xattrs)

```bash
pip install officework
git diff manuscripts/2026-09-report.adoc   # only the changed lines
getfattr -d document.docx                  # only if you use xattrs
```

== What the human holds

*Values the human supplies*

* The path of the document store
* The signing key shared between the editing engine and the gate
* The rule for who can open which documents (departments, roles)

*Actions the AI states before performing*

* Converting or moving existing documents in bulk
* Rewriting the access-control table
* Sending a file outside the company

== Versions checked, and when

* aiseed office (officework) engine 0.5.0. `pip install officework`. Published 2026-09-04, confirmed on PyPI on 2026-09-20
* The GUI application and AsciiDoc read/write are not shipped as of 2026-09-20
* Claude Docs shipped in beta on 2026-09-16, available on Pro, Max, Team, and Enterprise. Checked 2026-09-20
* Permissions live in PostgreSQL or the gate's SQLite
* This procedure was written on 2026-09-20
* If a version has moved, have the AI confirm the official procedure before proceeding

== Summary

Do not pick a storage place. Decide how the manuscript is held.

* *Manuscript as AsciiDoc text* — git gives a diff; the AI reads and writes it directly
* *Commit only the manuscript* — design lives elsewhere, so history carries only substance
* *Forms belong to aiseed office* — built because ONLYOFFICE does not hold Japanese forms. Engine at 0.5.0; the GUI and AsciiDoc support are not there yet
* *Claude Docs is a door* — drafting and co-editing are fast; export the finished manuscript as Markdown and bring it home
* *The files stay files* — gate for identity, structured store for permissions, no heavy storage app
* *The comparison is not against free* — compare against what you already pay a vendor

The next chapter puts the other large piece of clerical work, mail, on your own
side (2-08).

'''

== Related articles

* link:/en/ai-native-software/foundation/[2-03: Lay the Foundation — SQLite, PostgreSQL, pgvector, DuckDB, Polars]
* link:/en/ai-native-software/auth/[2-05: Stand Up the Gate — One Login with PocketBase]
* https://github.com/aiseed-dev/officework[aiseed office (officework) — a docx/xlsx engine that also typesets to PDF]
* https://github.com/aiseed-dev/workspace[Reference implementation kura — a self-hosted Microsoft 365 / Google Workspace alternative]
* link:/en/ai-native-software/independence/[2-01: Becoming Independent from Microsoft and Google — The Whole Map]
endif::[]
// ===== article: 13-mail =====
---
slug: mail
number: 08
part: 2
title.ja: メールを自分の側に ── Stalwart と Thunderbird
title.en: Mail on Your Own Side — Stalwart and Thunderbird
subtitle.ja: Exchange と Outlook の外へ ── 受信は自分で、送信は正直に
subtitle.en: Outside Exchange and Outlook — receive it yourself, send it honestly
description.ja: メールは事業の記録そのものだ。Stalwart は Rust 製の単一サーバで、SMTP・IMAP・JMAP・スパム対策・DKIM 署名を一つに備え、Exchange を置き換える。2-03 の PostgreSQL に乗せ、2-02 で AI に渡した一台の上に立て、Thunderbird など任意のクライアントで読む。ただし送信の到達性は難しい ── そこは認証付き中継に頼り、メールボックスの主導権だけ自分が持つ、という正直な設計を示す。DNS(MX・SPF・DKIM・DMARC・PTR)と外へのメール送信は、人が通す。
description.en: Mail is the record of the business itself. Stalwart is a Rust single server carrying SMTP, IMAP, JMAP, spam defense, and DKIM signing in one, replacing Exchange. It rides on the PostgreSQL from 2-03, stands up on the machine handed to the AI in 2-02, and is read with any client like Thunderbird. But outbound deliverability is hard — so lean on an authenticated relay for sending and keep only control of the mailbox on your own side. An honest design. DNS (MX, SPF, DKIM, DMARC, PTR) and sending mail to the outside stay with the human.
date: 2026.07.09
label: Independence 8
title_html.ja: メールボックスを、<br><span class="accent">自分の側</span>に。
title_html.en: Put the mailbox<br>on <span class="accent">your own side</span>.
---
ifdef::lang-ja[]
= メールを自分の側に ── Stalwart と Thunderbird

メールは、事業の記録そのものだ。やり取りも、契約も、履歴も、すべてそこに
積まれている。前章までで、土台・門番・コード・文書を自分の側に置いた。この章
では、その記録が毎日流れ込む受信箱を、自分の側に取り戻す。ただし、メールは
正直に語るべき領域でもある。*受信は易しく、送信の到達性は難しい*。その線を
はっきり引いたうえで立てる。

== 受信箱の主導権を、自分の側に戻す

* *記録の主導権* ── 過去メールの保管・検索・書き出しを、自分のルールで決める
* *人数課金から降りる* ── メールボックスの数で月額が積み上がらない
* *門番と一つに* ── アカウントを 2-05 の認証と揃える

== Stalwart を一台で立てる

メールサーバは Stalwart である。Rust 製の単一のサーバに、SMTP・IMAP・JMAP・
スパム対策・DKIM 署名まで入っている。Exchange の置き換えだ。保管は 2-03 の
PostgreSQL に寄せられる。立てる先は、2-02 で AI に渡した一台でよい。

```yaml
# compose.yaml ── 単一のメールサーバを立てる
services:
  mail:
    image: stalwartlabs/mail-server:latest
    volumes: ["./stalwart:/opt/stalwart-mail"]
    ports: ["25:25", "587:587", "465:465", "993:993", "8080:8080"]
    restart: always
```

管理画面(`:8080`)でドメインとメールボックスを作る。コードは書かない。

== DNS を正しく置く ── MX・SPF・DKIM・DMARC

メールは、立てるより *DNS が本体*だ。次の四つを正しく置く。

```dns
example.com.      MX    10 mail.example.com.
example.com.      TXT   "v=spf1 mx -all"
default._domainkey.example.com.  TXT  "v=DKIM1; k=rsa; p=...(Stalwart が生成)"
_dmarc.example.com.  TXT  "v=DMARC1; p=quarantine; rua=mailto:dmarc@example.com"
```

逆引き(PTR)も、サーバの IP に対して設定する。ここを外すと、送ったメールが
相手に届かない。

== 送信の到達性は、中継に借りる

自前サーバからの直接送信は、IP 評価・ブロックリスト・各社の判定に左右される。
届かないことがある。これは OSS の優劣ではなく、メールという仕組みの現実だ。

だから、現実的な設計はこうする。

* *受信・保管・検索* ── 自分の Stalwart が持つ。ここが主導権の本体である
* *外向きの送信* ── 認証付きの SMTP 中継(送信専用サービス)を経由する

中継は、送信の到達性だけを借りる薄い層だ。メールボックスそのものは自分の側に
残る。主導権を握る場所と、到達性を借りる場所を分ける。これが正直で堅実な
やり方だ。

____
メールは「全部自前」が正解とは限らない。
受信箱は自分の側に、送信の到達性は借りる ── 線を引いて立てる。
____

== 読み書きは Thunderbird でよい

読み書きは、IMAP / JMAP を話す任意のクライアントでよい。Thunderbird なら
無料で、Windows・Mac・Linux で同じように動く。スマホは標準のメールアプリが
そのまま JMAP / IMAP でつながる。

== Microsoft 365 からは並行で移す

既存のメールは、IMAP 同士をまるごと同期する imapsync で運ぶ。

```bash
imapsync --host1 outlook.office365.com --user1 you@old \
         --host2 mail.example.com      --user2 you@new
```

一斉に切り替えなくてよい。しばらく両方で受け、転送をかけて並行運用する。
落ち着いてから MX を Stalwart に向ける(2-12)。

移行の手順は Gmail でも同じだ。imapsync は IMAP 同士をつなぐ道具であって、
相手が Microsoft でも Google でも変わらない。

== 保存も安全も、決まりごとは数行

メールの保存に、専用のアーカイブ製品は要らない。メールは全部 2-03 の
PostgreSQL にあり、2-01 の「守るのはデータと仕様」の複製対象に含まれている。
何年残すかは、業務ルールの Markdown に一行書く。保存の仕様は、それで全部だ。

安全側も一行で済む。実行ファイルの添付は、開かない。スパムは Stalwart が
内蔵で弾く。疑わしいメールは、添付を開く前に本文を AI に読ませて、判断の
材料にする。

== 確かめ方

この章は、次の五つができていれば済みだ。

. 外のアドレス(Gmail など)から自分のドメイン宛に送ったメールが、Thunderbird で読める
. Thunderbird から外のアドレス宛に送ったメールが、相手の受信箱に届く
. 管理画面(`:8080`)に入れて、作ったドメインとメールボックスが見える
. 外から引いて、MX・SPF・DKIM・DMARC・PTR の五つが返る
. imapsync で運んだ過去のメールが、Thunderbird の中に見える

```bash
dig +short MX example.com                      # MX が引ける
dig +short TXT default._domainkey.example.com  # DKIM が引ける
dig +short -x <サーバの IP>                     # 逆引き(PTR)が引ける
```

== 人が持つ物

*人が渡す値*

* メールのドメイン名と、メールサーバのホスト名
* サーバの固定 IP と、逆引き(PTR)を設定してもらう先
* 管理画面の最初の管理者の名前とパスワード
* 送信に使う SMTP 中継のアカウントと認証情報
* 旧サーバ(Microsoft 365 など)の、imapsync に渡すアカウントとパスワード
* メールを何年残すか

*AI が「やる前に言う」操作*

* DNS のレコードを変える。とくに MX を Stalwart に向ける切り替え
* 外のアドレス宛にメールを送る
* imapsync を旧サーバに対して走らせる
* メールボックスを消す

== 確かめた版と日付

* Stalwart(`stalwartlabs/mail-server:latest`)、PostgreSQL、Thunderbird、imapsync
* 手順を書いたのは 2026-07-09
* 版が上がっていたら、AI に公式の手順を確かめさせてから進める

== まとめ

受信箱を、自分の側に。

* *Stalwart* ── SMTP・IMAP・JMAP・スパム対策・DKIM を単一サーバで(2-03 の PostgreSQL に乗る)
* *DNS(MX・SPF・DKIM・DMARC・PTR)* ── メールの本体はここ
* *送信は中継に頼る* ── 受信箱は自分、到達性は借りる、と線を引く
* *Thunderbird* ── 無料・全 OS 共通のクライアント
* *imapsync* ── Microsoft 365 から並行移行、最後に MX を向ける

次章では、会議と予約(Jitsi・Cal.com)を立て、Teams と予約サービスを自分の側に
置く。

'''

== 関連記事

* link:/ai-native-software/foundation/[2-03: 土台を据える ── SQLite・PostgreSQL・pgvector・DuckDB・Polars]
* link:/ai-native-software/auth/[2-05: 門番を立てる ── PocketBase で認証を一つに]
* link:/ai-native-software/independence/[2-01: Microsoft と Google から自立する ── 全体像と対応表]
endif::[]
ifdef::lang-en[]
= Mail on Your Own Side — Stalwart and Thunderbird

Mail is the record of the business itself. Correspondence, contracts, history —
all of it piles up there. By now the foundation, the gate, the code, and the
documents are on your own side. This chapter takes back the inbox those records
flow into every day. But mail is also a domain to be honest about. *Receiving is
easy; sending — deliverability — is hard.* Stand it up with that line drawn
clearly.

== Take control of the inbox back to your own side

* *Control of the record* — retention, search, and export of past mail by your own rules
* *Step off per-seat billing* — the monthly bill does not stack with mailbox count
* *One with the gate* — align accounts with the 2-05 authentication

== Stand up Stalwart on one machine

The mail server is Stalwart. A Rust single server carries SMTP, IMAP, JMAP,
spam defense, and DKIM signing. It is a replacement for Exchange. Storage can be
pointed at the PostgreSQL from 2-03. The machine it stands up on is the one
handed to the AI in 2-02.

```yaml
# compose.yaml — stand up a single mail server
services:
  mail:
    image: stalwartlabs/mail-server:latest
    volumes: ["./stalwart:/opt/stalwart-mail"]
    ports: ["25:25", "587:587", "465:465", "993:993", "8080:8080"]
    restart: always
```

In the admin UI (`:8080`), create the domain and the mailboxes. No code is
written.

== Get the DNS right — MX, SPF, DKIM, DMARC

With mail, the *DNS is the real body* of the work, more than the server. Set
these four correctly.

```dns
example.com.      MX    10 mail.example.com.
example.com.      TXT   "v=spf1 mx -all"
default._domainkey.example.com.  TXT  "v=DKIM1; k=rsa; p=...(generated by Stalwart)"
_dmarc.example.com.  TXT  "v=DMARC1; p=quarantine; rua=mailto:dmarc@example.com"
```

Set the reverse record (PTR) for the server's IP as well. Miss this, and the
mail you send does not reach anyone.

== Borrow outbound deliverability from a relay

Direct sending from a self-hosted server is at the mercy of IP reputation,
blocklists, and each provider's judgment. Sometimes it does not arrive. This is
not about OSS being inferior. It is the reality of how mail works.

So the realistic design is this.

* *Receiving, storage, search* — owned by your Stalwart, and this is the real body of control
* *Outbound sending* — go through an authenticated SMTP relay, a send-only service

The relay is a thin layer that borrows only sending deliverability. The mailbox
itself stays on your side. Separate where you hold control from where you borrow
deliverability. That is the honest, durable way.

____
"All in-house" is not always the right answer for mail.
The inbox on your side, the sending deliverability borrowed — stand it up with
the line drawn.
____

== Read and write with Thunderbird

Reading and writing work with any client that speaks IMAP / JMAP. Thunderbird is
free and runs the same on Windows, Mac, and Linux. Phones connect with the stock
mail app over JMAP / IMAP.

== Migrate from Microsoft 365 in parallel

Existing mail moves with imapsync, which syncs one IMAP store wholesale into
another.

```bash
imapsync --host1 outlook.office365.com --user1 you@old \
         --host2 mail.example.com      --user2 you@new
```

There is no need to cut over at once. Receive on both for a while, set
forwarding, and run in parallel. Point the MX at Stalwart once things have
settled (2-12).

Migration is the same from Gmail. imapsync connects IMAP to IMAP, and it does
not care whether the far side is Microsoft or Google.

== Retention and safety are a few lines of rules

Mail retention needs no dedicated archive product. All the mail already sits in
the 2-03 PostgreSQL, and it is already inside the 2-01 rule "protect the data
and the spec." How many years to keep is one line in the business-rules
Markdown. That is the entire retention spec.

The safety side is one line too. Never open executable attachments. Spam is
handled by Stalwart's built-in filter. For a suspicious mail, have the AI read
the body before any attachment is opened, as input to your judgment.

== How to check you are done

This chapter is done when these five hold.

. A mail sent from an outside address, such as Gmail, to your own domain can be read in Thunderbird
. A mail sent from Thunderbird to an outside address lands in that person's inbox
. You can log in to the admin UI (`:8080`) and see the domain and mailboxes you created
. Queried from outside, all five records answer: MX, SPF, DKIM, DMARC, PTR
. Past mail carried over by imapsync is visible inside Thunderbird

```bash
dig +short MX example.com                      # the MX answers
dig +short TXT default._domainkey.example.com  # the DKIM key answers
dig +short -x <the server's IP>                # the reverse record (PTR) answers
```

== What the human holds

*Values the human supplies*

* The mail domain name and the mail server's hostname
* The server's fixed IP, and whoever sets the reverse record (PTR) for it
* The name and password of the first administrator in the admin UI
* The account and credentials of the SMTP relay used for sending
* The account and password on the old server, such as Microsoft 365, handed to imapsync
* How many years mail is kept

*Actions the AI states before performing*

* Changing a DNS record, above all switching the MX to Stalwart
* Sending mail to an outside address
* Running imapsync against the old server
* Deleting a mailbox

== Versions checked, and when

* Stalwart (`stalwartlabs/mail-server:latest`), PostgreSQL, Thunderbird, imapsync
* This procedure was written on 2026-07-09
* If a version has moved, have the AI confirm the official procedure before proceeding

== Summary

The inbox, on your own side.

* *Stalwart* — SMTP, IMAP, JMAP, spam defense, DKIM in one server (on the 2-03 PostgreSQL)
* *DNS (MX, SPF, DKIM, DMARC, PTR)* — the real body of mail is here
* *Lean on a relay for sending* — inbox yours, deliverability borrowed, line drawn
* *Thunderbird* — a free client, identical across OSes
* *imapsync* — migrate from Microsoft 365 in parallel, point the MX last

The next chapter stands up meetings and booking (Jitsi, Cal.com), bringing Teams
and the booking services to our own side.

'''

== Related articles

* link:/en/ai-native-software/foundation/[2-03: Lay the Foundation — SQLite, PostgreSQL, pgvector, DuckDB, Polars]
* link:/en/ai-native-software/auth/[2-05: Stand Up the Gate — One Login with PocketBase]
* link:/en/ai-native-software/independence/[2-01: Becoming Independent from Microsoft and Google — The Whole Map]
endif::[]
// ===== article: 14-meetings =====
---
slug: meetings
number: 09
part: 2
title.ja: 会議と予約を自分の側に ── Jitsi と Cal.com
title.en: Meetings and Booking on Your Own Side — Jitsi and Cal.com
subtitle.ja: Teams の会議も、Calendly の予約も、講座のウェビナーも ── 自分のドメインで
subtitle.en: Teams meetings, Calendly booking, and webinars for classes — on your own domain
description.ja: ビデオ会議は Jitsi、予約は Cal.com、講座・ウェビナーは BigBlueButton。Teams・Zoom・Calendly・Microsoft Bookings を、自分のドメインと 2-02 で AI に渡した一台に置き換える。予約は 2-03 の PostgreSQL に乗り、2-08 のメールで確認を送る。人数・分課金から降り、会議のリンクも記録も自分の側に持つ。人が持つのは、ドメインと DNS、予約の枠、外へ出る確認メールの三つだ。
description.en: Video meetings with Jitsi, booking with Cal.com, classes and webinars with BigBlueButton. Replace Teams, Zoom, Calendly, and Microsoft Bookings with your own domain on the machine handed to the AI in 2-02. Booking rides on the PostgreSQL from 2-03 and sends confirmations through the 2-08 mail. Step off per-seat, per-minute billing and keep meeting links and records on your own side. The human holds the domain and DNS, the booking slots, and any confirmation mail that goes outside.
date: 2026.07.11
label: Independence 9
title_html.ja: 会議も予約も、<br><span class="accent">自分のドメイン</span>で。
title_html.en: Meetings and booking,<br>on <span class="accent">your own domain</span>.
---
ifdef::lang-ja[]
= 会議と予約を自分の側に ── Jitsi と Cal.com

会議も予約も、汎用の機能だ。Teams の打ち合わせ、Calendly の日程調整、
Microsoft Bookings の講座予約 ── どれも、すでに OSS で共有されている。前章で
メールを自分の側に戻した。ここでは三つを立て、2-02 で AI に渡した一台と、自分
のドメインの上に置く。

* *ビデオ会議* ── Jitsi Meet(Teams・Zoom の代わり)
* *予約* ── Cal.com(Calendly・Bookings の代わり)
* *講座・ウェビナー* ── BigBlueButton(大人数の授業向け)

== 会議は Jitsi Meet で立てる

少人数から中人数の会議は Jitsi Meet である。*参加者はブラウザだけ*でよい。
アプリのインストールも、アカウントも要らない。リンクを送れば会議が始まる。

```bash
# 公式の compose 一式を使う
git clone https://github.com/jitsi/docker-jitsi-meet
cd docker-jitsi-meet && cp env.example .env && ./gen-passwords.sh
docker compose up -d        # web・prosody・jicofo・jvb が立つ
```

`meet.example.com` を、自分のロゴと自分のドメインで配れる。録画もチャットも
画面共有も、標準で揃う。

____
大人数(数百人)の会議は、映像中継(JVB)を増設して捌く。これは
「立てる」より「足す」── 規模に応じてサーバを並べる作業だ。
____

== 講座は BigBlueButton で開く

セミナーや授業のように、ホワイトボード・挙手・ブレイクアウト・出席が要る場面
は BigBlueButton である。教育向けに作られたウェビナー基盤で、2-03 で触れた
「講座」のための道具だ。

BigBlueButton は専用のインストーラを持ち、Jitsi より重い。*日常会議は Jitsi、
講座は BigBlueButton*、と用途で分けて立てる。

== 予約は Cal.com に乗せる

日程調整・講座予約は Cal.com である。Calendly と Microsoft Bookings の置き換え
で、データは 2-03 の PostgreSQL に乗る。

```yaml
# compose.yaml ── 予約を 2-03 の DB に乗せて立てる
services:
  booking:
    image: calcom/cal.com:latest
    environment:
      DATABASE_URL: postgres://postgres:change-me@db:5432/calcom
      NEXT_PUBLIC_WEBAPP_URL: https://book.example.com
    restart: always
```

予約が入れば、2-08 のメールで確認と前日リマインドが飛び、Jitsi の会議リンクが
自動で添えられる。土台(DB)・門番(認証)・メールの上に、予約がそのまま乗る。
先に据えたものが、ここで効いてくる。

== カレンダーは CalDAV で同期し、門番の内側に置く

予定の同期は CalDAV である。軽量な Radicale を一つ立てれば、Thunderbird や
スマホの標準カレンダーとつながる。Cal.com と各アプリは、2-05 の門番の内側、
リバースプロキシの先に置く。

```caddy
meet.example.com { reverse_proxy jitsi-web:80 }
book.example.com { reverse_proxy booking:3000 }
```

== 移行は、未来の予定だけでいい

Teams や Google カレンダーからの移行に、プロジェクトは要らない。会議のリンク
は使い捨てで、移すものが無い。カレンダーは*未来の予定だけ*を .ics で書き出して
Radicale に読み込む。一度きりの操作だ。過去の録画やチャットに残す価値のある
ものは、2-15 の情報の整備で棚卸しして、ファイルとして 2-07 の置き場に入れる。

== 確かめ方

この章は、次の五つができていれば済みだ。

. 別の PC のブラウザから `meet.example.com` の会議に入れる。アプリもアカウントも要らない
. 二人以上で入って、映像・音声・画面共有が通る
. `book.example.com` で予約を入れると、確認のメールが届き、Jitsi のリンクが付いている
. Thunderbird かスマホの標準カレンダーが、CalDAV でその予定を表示する
. 講座を立てたなら、BigBlueButton でホワイトボードと挙手が動く

```bash
curl -sI https://meet.example.com | head -1   # 会議の頁が返る
curl -sI https://book.example.com | head -1   # 予約の頁が返る
```

== 人が持つ物

*人が渡す値*

* 会議と予約のドメイン名(`meet.example.com`・`book.example.com`)
* その名前を指す DNS の設定
* Cal.com がつなぐ DB の接続先とパスワード
* 予約の受付時間と、一枠の長さ
* 確認メールの差出人アドレス(2-08 のメール)
* 移す未来の予定の .ics

*AI が「やる前に言う」操作*

* DNS のレコードを変える
* 予約の確認メールを、外のアドレスへ実際に送る
* 既に入っている予約のデータを消す
* 会議の録画を、外から見える場所に置く

== 確かめた版と日付

* Jitsi Meet(`docker-jitsi-meet` の compose 一式)、BigBlueButton、Cal.com(`calcom/cal.com:latest`)、Radicale、Caddy、PostgreSQL
* 手順を書いたのは 2026-07-11
* 版が上がっていたら、AI に公式の手順を確かめさせてから進める

== まとめ

会議も予約も、自分のドメインで。

* *Jitsi Meet* ── ブラウザだけの会議、Teams・Zoom の代わり
* *BigBlueButton* ── ホワイトボードと出席のある講座・ウェビナー
* *Cal.com* ── Calendly・Bookings の代わり(2-03 の PostgreSQL に乗る)
* *連携* ── 予約 → 2-08 のメールで確認、Jitsi リンクを自動添付
* *CalDAV と門番* ── 標準カレンダーと同期、リバースプロキシの内側に

ここまでで、土台・門番・文書・コード・メール・会議・予約が揃った。次章では、
これらを社外に見せる Web サイトを作る。

'''

== 関連記事

* link:/ai-native-software/foundation/[2-03: 土台を据える ── SQLite・PostgreSQL・pgvector・DuckDB・Polars]
* link:/ai-native-software/mail/[2-08: メールを自分の側に ── Stalwart と Thunderbird]
* link:/ai-native-software/independence/[2-01: Microsoft と Google から自立する ── 全体像と対応表]
endif::[]
ifdef::lang-en[]
= Meetings and Booking on Your Own Side — Jitsi and Cal.com

Meetings and booking are generic functions. Teams calls, Calendly scheduling,
Microsoft Bookings for course reservations — all of it is already shared as OSS.
The previous chapter brought mail back to your own side. Here we stand up three
things and place them on your own domain, on the machine handed to the AI in
2-02.

* *Video meetings* — Jitsi Meet (in place of Teams and Zoom)
* *Booking* — Cal.com (in place of Calendly and Bookings)
* *Classes and webinars* — BigBlueButton (for larger teaching sessions)

== Stand up Jitsi Meet for meetings

For small-to-mid meetings, Jitsi Meet. Participants need *only a browser* — no
app install, no account. Send a link and the meeting starts.

```bash
# use the official compose bundle
git clone https://github.com/jitsi/docker-jitsi-meet
cd docker-jitsi-meet && cp env.example .env && ./gen-passwords.sh
docker compose up -d        # web, prosody, jicofo, jvb come up
```

Hand out `meet.example.com` with your own logo and your own domain. Recording,
chat, and screen sharing all come standard.

____
Large meetings (hundreds) are handled by adding video bridges (JVB). That is
"adding" more than "standing up" — lining up servers to match scale.
____

== Run classes on BigBlueButton

Where you need a whiteboard, raise-hand, breakout rooms, and attendance — in
seminars and lessons — use BigBlueButton. It is a webinar platform built for
education, the tool for the "courses" mentioned back in 2-03.

BigBlueButton has its own installer and is heavier than Jitsi. *Day-to-day
meetings on Jitsi, classes on BigBlueButton* — stand each up for its purpose.

== Put booking on Cal.com

Scheduling and course booking run on Cal.com, a replacement for Calendly and
Microsoft Bookings, with data on the PostgreSQL from 2-03.

```yaml
# compose.yaml — stand up booking on the 2-03 DB
services:
  booking:
    image: calcom/cal.com:latest
    environment:
      DATABASE_URL: postgres://postgres:change-me@db:5432/calcom
      NEXT_PUBLIC_WEBAPP_URL: https://book.example.com
    restart: always
```

When a booking lands, the 2-08 mail sends a confirmation and a day-before
reminder, with the Jitsi meeting link attached automatically. On top of the
foundation (DB), the gate (auth), and mail, booking just sits. What was laid
first pays off here.

== Sync the calendar over CalDAV, behind the gate

Schedule sync is CalDAV. Stand up one lightweight Radicale and it connects to
Thunderbird and the stock phone calendar. Cal.com and each app sit behind the
2-05 gate, beyond the reverse proxy.

```caddy
meet.example.com { reverse_proxy jitsi-web:80 }
book.example.com { reverse_proxy booking:3000 }
```

== Migration means future events only

Migrating off Teams or Google Calendar needs no project. Meeting links are
disposable, so there is nothing to carry over. For the calendar, export *future
events only* as .ics and load them into Radicale. It is a one-time operation.
Whatever in the old recordings and chats is worth keeping gets triaged in the
2-15 information-preparation pass and filed into the 2-07 store.

== How to check you are done

This chapter is done when these five hold.

. From a browser on a different PC, you can join a meeting at `meet.example.com`, with no app and no account
. With two or more people in the room, video, audio, and screen sharing get through
. Making a booking at `book.example.com` produces a confirmation mail carrying the Jitsi link
. Thunderbird or the stock phone calendar shows that booking over CalDAV
. If you stood up classes, the whiteboard and raise-hand work in BigBlueButton

```bash
curl -sI https://meet.example.com | head -1   # the meeting page answers
curl -sI https://book.example.com | head -1   # the booking page answers
```

== What the human holds

*Values the human supplies*

* The domain names for meetings and booking (`meet.example.com`, `book.example.com`)
* The DNS entries that point those names at the machine
* The database Cal.com connects to, and its password
* The booking hours and the length of one slot
* The sender address on confirmation mail (the 2-08 mail)
* The .ics of future events to be carried over

*Actions the AI states before performing*

* Changing a DNS record
* Actually sending a confirmation mail to an outside address
* Deleting bookings that have already been made
* Putting a meeting recording somewhere visible from outside

== Versions checked, and when

* Jitsi Meet (the `docker-jitsi-meet` compose bundle), BigBlueButton, Cal.com (`calcom/cal.com:latest`), Radicale, Caddy, PostgreSQL
* This procedure was written on 2026-07-11
* If a version has moved, have the AI confirm the official procedure before proceeding

== Summary

Meetings and booking, on your own domain.

* *Jitsi Meet* — browser-only meetings, in place of Teams and Zoom
* *BigBlueButton* — classes and webinars with whiteboard and attendance
* *Cal.com* — in place of Calendly and Bookings (on the 2-03 PostgreSQL)
* *Integration* — booking, then confirmation via the 2-08 mail, Jitsi link auto-attached
* *CalDAV and the gate* — sync with stock calendars, behind the reverse proxy

By now the foundation, gate, documents, code, mail, meetings, and booking are in
place. Next, we build the website that shows these to the outside.

'''

== Related articles

* link:/en/ai-native-software/foundation/[2-03: Lay the Foundation — SQLite, PostgreSQL, pgvector, DuckDB, Polars]
* link:/en/ai-native-software/mail/[2-08: Mail on Your Own Side — Stalwart and Thunderbird]
* link:/en/ai-native-software/independence/[2-01: Becoming Independent from Microsoft and Google — The Whole Map]
endif::[]
// ===== article: 15-web-build =====
---
slug: web-build
number: 10
part: 2
title.ja: Web を作る ── HTML と CSS と JavaScript に戻る
title.en: Build the Web — Back to HTML, CSS, and JavaScript
subtitle.ja: 中身は Markdown と Mermaid、外枠は最小限の HTML と CSS
subtitle.en: Content in Markdown and Mermaid, the frame in minimal HTML and CSS
description.ja: Web サイトを二層に分けて組み立てる。中身は Markdown と Mermaid、外枠は HTML と CSS と最小限の JavaScript、両者を Python が繋ぐ。フレームワークとビルドツールの列から素の Web 標準に戻ると、依存が三つか四つに収まり、ビルドは数秒で終わる。WordPress に入っている中身は、七つの手順で Markdown に移せる。作り直す時間が無ければ、Playwright で今あるサイトを丸ごと静的に写し取る手もある。npm を離れるもう一つの理由はサプライチェーンにあり、動く部分は FastAPI を一つ選んで最小限に保つ。
description.en: Build a website in two layers. Content in Markdown and Mermaid, the frame in HTML, CSS, and minimal JavaScript, with Python connecting the two. Coming back from the row of frameworks and build tools to raw web standards leaves three or four dependencies and a build that finishes in seconds. Content held in WordPress moves to Markdown in seven steps, and where there is no time to rebuild, Playwright can mirror the existing site into static files. The other reason to leave npm is the supply chain, and the moving parts stay on one FastAPI, kept minimal.
date: 2026.09.21
label: Independence 10
title_html.ja: 中身は Markdown、<br>外枠だけ <span class="accent">HTML と CSS</span>。
title_html.en: Content in Markdown,<br>the frame only <span class="accent">HTML and CSS</span>.
---
ifdef::lang-ja[]
= Web を作る ── HTML と CSS と JavaScript に戻る

*Web サイトは、二層に分けて作る*。

link:/ai-native-software/meetings/[2-09: 会議と予約を自分の側に ── Jitsi と Cal.com]
で、集まりと予約を自分の側に置いた。ここでは外に見せる Web を、自分の手元で組み立てる。
中身は Markdown と Mermaid、外枠は HTML と CSS と最小限の JavaScript、両者を Python が
繋ぐ。焼き上がった HTML をどこに置くかは、次の章が受け持つ。

== React の疲れは、道具の数から来る

過去 10 年、Web 開発はフレームワークの軍拡だった。

jQuery、Backbone、Angular、React、Vue、Svelte。ビルドツールは Grunt、Gulp、Webpack、
Rollup、Vite、Turbopack。CSS は Sass、Less、PostCSS、Tailwind、CSS-in-JS。サーバー側の
描画は Next.js、Nuxt、Remix、SvelteKit、Astro。

どれも何かの問題を解いてきた。しかし、解いた問題と新しく生まれた問題を並べると、
割に合わない場面が増えた。

* ビルド設定の習得に数週間かかる
* 依存パッケージが 1,000 個を超える
* セキュリティ更新が毎週来る
* 一年後にビルドが通らなくなる
* 簡単なサイト一つに 100 MB のフォルダが要る

これは技術そのものの複雑さではなく、自分たちで足した複雑さだ。素の HTML と CSS と
JavaScript に戻ると、この五つが一度に片づく。ブラウザはこの三つを直接読むので、
間に変換の層が要らない。そして AI が最も安定して書けるのも、この三つと Markdown だ。

== WordPress には四つの罠がある

「React は使っていない、うちは WordPress だ」── 多くの人がそう思っているはずだ。

それは事実に合っている。世界の Web サイトの約 43% が WordPress で動いている。日本でも
企業の公式サイト、個人ブログ、ニュースメディア、自治体の Web ── あらゆる場所にある。

ただし WordPress には WordPress の罠がある。React の罠とは質が違い、深刻さは同じか、
それ以上だ。

*罠 1: 中身が MySQL に入る*

WordPress の記事は、テキストファイルではなく、MySQL の中の HTML 混じりのレコードとして
保存される。同じ記事を PDF にしたい、別のサイトに移したい、AI に渡して分析したい ──
どの道もエクスポート作業から始まる。

しかもエクスポート形式は `.xml`(WordPress 独自の WXR)で、HTML タグや独自の短縮記法
(`[gallery]` など)が混ざっている。中身が WordPress の側に残る。これは
link:/ai-native-software/foundation/[2-03: 土台を据える ── SQLite・PostgreSQL・pgvector・DuckDB・Polars]
で見た「Excel に入ったままのデータ」と同じ形だ。

*罠 2: プラグインが攻撃面を増やす*

WordPress の機能拡張は、世界中の有志が作ったプラグインに頼る。一つのサイトに 20〜30 の
プラグインが入っているのは普通だ。それぞれが定期的に脆弱性を出す。サイト全体が攻撃面の
集合になる。世界の Web の 43% が同じ装置で動くということは、攻撃する側から見れば、
43% の標的が同じ装置だということでもある。

*罠 3: 更新が互いを止める*

WordPress 本体、テーマ、プラグイン ── これらが互いに依存している。一つを更新すると、
別のものが動かなくなる。「更新を当てたらサイトが落ちた」は、WordPress の運用でよく
起きることだ。

*罠 4: PHP より Python のほうが、AI の出力が安定する*

WordPress のテーマとプラグインは PHP で書かれている。Claude は PHP も書けるが、
出力の安定度は Python のほうが高い。AI と組んで道具を作るなら、Python の側に寄せた
ほうが速い。

とくに急ぐのは、ネットショップだ。決済と顧客の個人情報を扱うため、価値の高い標的に
なる ── カード情報の抜き取り、個人情報の流出。被害が直接、お金と信用に響く。
WordPress のネットショップは、まっ先に静的にするとよい。決済は Stripe などの外部の
決済ページに任せ、カード情報を自分で持たない。動く PHP・データベース・管理ログインを
手放せば、攻撃面ごと消える。

== WordPress からは、七つの手順で抜ける

WordPress を使っているなら、抜け道は決まっている。
link:/ai-native-software/fastapi/[2-12: API を作る ── FastAPI で基幹のロジックを出す]
で扱う *並行稼働* と同じ形で移る。

. 既存の WordPress を動かしたまま、中身を Markdown にエクスポートする(プラグインや CLI ツールで自動化できる。変換コードは Claude が書く)
. 新しいサイトを Markdown + 最小限の HTML/CSS + Python で組む
. 同じ URL 構造を保つ(検索の評価を引き継ぐ)
. ステージング環境で動きを確認し、検索エンジンへのインデックス申請を用意する
. DNS を切り替える日を決める
. 切り替えの後、WordPress を読み取り専用にして 1 か月動かす
. 問題が出なければ WordPress を止め、ホスティング契約を解く

費用の差も大きい。WordPress のマネージドホスティング(WP Engine、Kinsta など)は月額
数千〜数万円かかる。静的 HTML を Cloudflare Pages や GitHub Pages に置けば、月額はゼロに
なる。年間で数万〜数十万円が浮く。

____
WordPress も並行稼働で抜ける。Markdown へエクスポートし、外枠だけ HTML と CSS で書き、
Python で生成して、静的に配る。これで WordPress を止められる。
____

作り直す時間が無ければ、もっと速い手もある ── *今あるサイトを丸ごと静的に写し取る*。
ヘッドレスブラウザ(Playwright)で各ページを開き、HTML と、読み込む全ファイル
(CSS・JS・画像・フォント)を保存し、参照を相対パスに書き換える。既定では JavaScript を
実行せず、サーバーが返す素の HTML を保存する ── 広告や解析ウィジェットが描画時に足す
膨大な DOM が入らない、軽い HTML になる。本文を JS で組み立てるサイトのときだけ、
描画を有効にすればいい。どちらにせよブラウザはページが実際に読み込むファイルを残らず
辿って落とすので、`requests` や `wget` のような取りこぼしがない。これを静的配信に
載せれば、動く WordPress を攻撃面ごと止められる。Mac の SiteSucker(有料)と同じことが、
AI に頼めば Python で書ける(このサイトのリポジトリには `tools/mirror_site.py` がある)。

== 中身と外枠を、二層に分ける

Web サイトの中身は、二つに分けられる。

|===
| 種類 | 何を書くか | 道具
| 中身 | 文章、表、引用、コード、図 | Markdown + Mermaid
| 外枠 | ヘッダー、ナビ、フッター、レイアウト、配色 | HTML + CSS + 最小限の JavaScript
| 接続 | 中身を外枠に流し込んで HTML を生成 | Python
|===

これを混ぜて書くのが、これまでの Web 制作の引っかかりだった。React のコンポーネントには、
文章と書式とロジックが一緒に入っている。WordPress の記事には、HTML タグと文章が一緒に
入っている。後から書き換えるとき、この混在が効いてくる。

AI ネイティブな分け方は、中身と外枠を完全に離すことだ。中身は Markdown と Mermaid だけで
書き、HTML タグは置かない。外枠は HTML と CSS で書き、各記事の中身には触らない。Python が
機械的に繋ぐ。

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Content["中身(別の出口にも回せる)"]
    MD["記事 *.md<br/>(Markdown)"]
    MMD["図 *.mmd<br/>(Mermaid)"]
  end
  subgraph Frame["外枠"]
    HTML["template.html"]
    CSS["style.css"]
    JS["main.js(最小)"]
  end
  Py(("Python<br/>ビルド"))

  MD --> Py
  MMD --> Py
  HTML --> Py
  CSS --> Py
  JS --> Py
  Py --> Site["静的 HTML サイト"]

  MD -.->|そのまま| PDF["PDF / 印刷"]
  MD -.->|そのまま| AI[("AI 分析")]
  MD -.->|そのまま| Book["電子書籍"]

  classDef good fill:#e8f5e9,stroke:#7a9a6d,color:#3a4d34
  classDef bad  fill:#fef3e7,stroke:#c89559,color:#5a3f1a
  class MD,MMD good
  class HTML,CSS,JS bad
```

== 中身を Markdown で持つと、出口が増える

中身を Markdown と Mermaid で書く最大の理由は、同じデータが Web 以外の出口にも回ることだ。

同じ Markdown ファイルから、

* Web ページが作れる(Python が HTML 化する)
* PDF が作れる(`pandoc` でも Claude でも変換できる)
* 印刷用の原稿に変換できる
* 電子書籍(EPUB)が作れる
* AI に渡して要約・翻訳・質問ができる
* 別の媒体への貼り付けに回せる
* Git で差分を見て、履歴を持てる
* 他の人と一緒に編集できる

中身が Web の側に残らない。WordPress や Wix で書いた中身は、サービスが終わるとそこまでだ。
Notion で書いた中身は、Notion の形式の中にある。Markdown は、どこにも属さない。

これは link:/ai-native-software/documents/[2-07: 文書を取り戻す ── 原稿は adoc、様式は aiseed office]
で見た「中身は素のテキストで持つ」考え方の、Web 版だ。入口・中身・出口を分ける。

* 入口 ── 画像、PDF、音声、Word から、Claude がテキストに起こす
* 中身 ── Markdown と Mermaid で持ち、Git で版を管理する
* 出口 ── Web、PDF、印刷、AI 分析、電子書籍。用途に応じて Python が変換する

== 外枠は最小限で書く

外枠 ── ヘッダー、ナビ、フッター、レイアウト、配色 ── は、HTML と CSS で直接書く。
最小限で足りる。

* HTML テンプレートは 1〜数ファイル
* CSS は 1 ファイル(数百〜数千行)
* JavaScript は本当に要る所(モバイルメニューの開閉など)だけ、数十行

これらは年に数回しか触らない。外枠に時間を掛けすぎない。骨格は骨格として置き、時間は
中身に使う。

*手元に置く物*

* HTML(構造)── テンプレート 1 ファイル
* CSS(見た目)── 1 ファイル
* JavaScript(動く所だけ)── 数十行
* Markdown + Mermaid(中身)── 記事ごとに 1 ファイル

*要らなくなる物*

* JavaScript フレームワーク(React、Vue、Angular、Svelte)
* ビルドツール(Webpack、Vite、Turbopack、Parcel)
* TypeScript(素の JS で足りる)
* CSS フレームワーク(Tailwind、Bootstrap)
* パッケージマネージャ(npm、yarn、pnpm)と `node_modules`

残るのは、素の Web 標準と Markdown だけだ。HTML / CSS / JS には変換の層が無く、Markdown は
AI が最もよく扱う記法だ。どちらも、Claude が出した物をそのまま置ける。

== npm を離れるもう一つの理由は、サプライチェーンにある

ビルドが遅いこと、依存が重いことだけが理由ではない。npm を離れるもう一つの理由は、
*レジストリを通して他人のコードが実行される* という構造にある。

npm レジストリでは、サプライチェーンを狙う攻撃が繰り返し起きている。似た名前の
パッケージで誤インストールを誘うタイポスクワッティング、依存パッケージ経由のコード注入、
メンテナーのアカウント侵害によるパッケージの乗っ取り、ポストインストールスクリプトでの
秘密情報の流出 ── 確かめられる代表例だけでも、こう並ぶ。

* 2018 年:`event-stream` 事件(暗号通貨ウォレット狙い)
* 2021 年:`ua-parser-js` 事件(マイニング・情報窃取のマルウェア注入)
* 2022 年:`colors`、`faker` 事件(作者自身による破壊コミット)
* 2024 年:`xz-utils` のバックドア(npm ではないが、長期のメンテナー乗っ取りの典型例)

ほぼ毎年、大きな事件が報告されている。大手企業が公式に配布する npm パッケージが、
サプライチェーン経由で攻撃の踏み台になった例も繰り返し起きている。個別の事件は、
link:/ai-native-software/verify-narratives/[3-02: AI で物語を検証する]
の手順で、各自が一次情報に当たって確かめてほしい。

=== この構造は、npm の設計から来ている

問題は、npm の生態系の設計そのものにある。

* 一つのプロジェクトが数百〜数千のパッケージを推移的に依存する
* それらのパッケージは、世界中の個人メンテナーが管理している
* 更新は自動で取られる(`npm install` で最新が来る)
* パッケージマネージャは依存先のコードを検査せずに実行する
* ビルド手順や postinstall フックで、任意のコードが走る

このうち一つが乗っ取られれば、取り込んだプロジェクト全体に届く。1 個のパッケージが、
推移的に 1,000 個のリポジトリに影響するという形は、npm を使う限り付いてくる。この
構造がどう効いてくるかは link:/ai-native-software/lockin/[3-05: ロックイン問題]
の話と噛み合う。

これに対して、この章のスタックは依存が桁違いに少ない。Markdown + HTML + CSS + JS は
外部依存ゼロ(ブラウザ標準だけ)、Python のビルドスクリプトは `markdown-it-py`、
`Jinja2`、`Pillow` の 3 個だ。3 個なら、全てのソースを Claude と一緒に読み通せる規模に
なる。怪しい挙動があれば気付ける。1,000 個の依存ツリーは、人の目では追い切れない。
Python のパッケージは PyPI の重要なものほど検査が厳しく、インストール時に任意コードが
走る形も標準ではない。侵害される表面積が、桁違いに小さい。

=== 依存 1,000 個に AI を入れるということ

AI 時代に、もう一つ重い点がある。

Claude Code は、エディタの裏でファイルを読み、コマンドを実行する。開発機の
`node_modules` のどこかに悪意のあるパッケージが潜んでいれば、*AI 経由でその実行が
引き起こされる* 可能性がある。

____
依存を 1,000 個抱えるプロジェクトに AI を入れることは、信頼できない他人を 1,000 人、
自分の作業空間に招き入れることと同じだ。
____

依存の無いスタックなら、この心配がない。HTML、CSS、Markdown は、ただのテキストだ。
実行されるコードは、自分と Claude が書いた物だけになる。「軽い」「速い」だけでなく、
「侵害される入口が無い」── これが依存を減らすもう一つの理由だ。

== Python が中身と外枠を繋ぐ

Markdown を HTML に、Mermaid の図を SVG か画像に、そして外枠のテンプレートに流し込む ──
これは Python スクリプトの仕事だ。

```
articles/foo/ja.md  ──→  Python  ──→  html/foo/index.html
                              ↑
                      tools/templates/article.html(外枠)
```

このスクリプトを自分で書く必要は無い。Claude に頼んで書いてもらう。`markdown-it-py` で
Markdown を HTML にし、`Jinja2` でテンプレートに流し込む。両方合わせて 100〜200 行だ。

依存は三つか四つで収まる。

* `markdown-it-py` ── CommonMark のパーサ
* `Jinja2` ── HTML テンプレートエンジン
* `Pillow` ── 画像処理(OG 画像の生成など、要るときだけ)
* Mermaid の図は、ブラウザ側で描くか、`mermaid-cli` で SVG にする

`uv add markdown-it-py jinja2 pillow` を一度打てば、あとは `git clone && uv sync` で
他の機械でも動く。`node_modules` も `requirements.txt` も出てこない。

このサイト ── aiseed.dev ── のビルドスクリプト(`tools/build_article.py`)も、ほとんど
Claude が書いた。ビルドツールを自分の手元に持てる時代だ。フレームワークの規約に従う
代わりに、自分の規約で書く。図と資料の作り方は
link:/ai-native-software/design/[2-13: 図と資料を作る ── Mermaid・Marp・そのほかの道具]
に続く。

== 動く部分は、FastAPI を一つ選ぶ

「動的なサイトは作れないのでは」と思うかもしれない。動く部分はサーバー側に置く。
Python の FastAPI、これ一つでいい。

Flask、Django、Go、Rust、Ruby ── 選択肢は山ほどある。しかし選択肢を増やすと、組織は
そのぶん分かれる。「FastAPI で書こう」と決めれば、そこから先の議論が終わる。
*選んだら、選んだことを忘れる*。これが道具立ての考え方だ。

なぜ FastAPI か。

* Python なので、この連載のスタックと揃う
* 型ヒントと Pydantic が自然に使える
* async が標準に入っている
* OpenAPI のドキュメントが自動で出る
* Claude が最も書きやすい(オープンソースで学習データが多い)
* 定型の記述が少ない

そして、その FastAPI も最小限に保つ。

* ORM を使わない。PostgreSQL のドライバ(asyncpg または psycopg)で SQL を直接書く
* 層を積まない。Repository 層、Service 層、Domain 層を置かず、リクエストを受け、SQL を実行し、HTML を返す
* 依存を増やさない。FastAPI、PostgreSQL ドライバ、Jinja2(要るとき)の三つで足りる
* 設定ファイルを増やさない。環境変数だけで動かす
* 一つのプロセスで動かす

一つの関数の形は、こうなる。

```python
@app.get("/items", response_class=HTMLResponse)
async def items():
    rows = await conn.fetch("SELECT name, price FROM items")
    return render("items.html", rows=rows)
```

リクエストを受け、SQL を実行し、HTML を返す。ここから先は、これ以上の何が要るのかを
問い続ける。Claude が直接 SQL を書ける以上、ORM の抽象化を挟まなくても届く。書き方は
AI が知っているので、人が渡すのは決めたことのほうだ。

クライアント側の JavaScript も最小限でいい。リンク遷移は `<a>`、フォーム送信は
`<form>` で足りる。部分的な更新が要るなら HTMX(数 KB のライブラリで、フレームワーク
ではない)。WebSocket は本当に必要になったときに足す。最初から SPA を組まない。

FastAPI の書き方と、基幹のロジックを API として出す手順は
link:/ai-native-software/fastapi/[2-12: API を作る ── FastAPI で基幹のロジックを出す]
が受け持つ。ここでは、選ぶ理由までで足りる。

== この形で作れるもの

「中身は Markdown、外枠は HTML と CSS、Python が繋ぐ」は、実例で見るのが速い。

* *個人事業主のポートフォリオ* ── 作品ごとの `.md` と画像、テンプレート 1 ファイルと `style.css` 1 ファイル、Python のビルドは 50 行。30〜100 ページ、ビルド数秒、月額ゼロ
* *中小企業のコーポレートサイト* ── 会社概要・サービス・問い合わせ・ブログの Markdown。問い合わせフォームは FastAPI 50 行と SMTP、求人一覧は SQLite + FastAPI で 30 行。50〜200 ページ、月額千円未満
* *NPO のイベント告知* ── `events/2026-04-foo.md` のようなイベントごとの Markdown。年別の目次は Python が自動生成、申し込みは外部フォームへのリンクか FastAPI 30 行
* *学校の学級通信*(保護者限定)── 週次の Markdown と写真、学校の色で `style.css` 1 ファイル。Basic 認証(`htpasswd`)か、link:/ai-native-software/auth/[2-05: 門番を立てる ── PocketBase で認証を一つに] の門番の内側に置く
* *研究室の論文・データ公開* ── 論文の原稿と、データセットのメタデータ。データは Parquet のまま直接ダウンロードさせる

共通するのは、依存パッケージが 3〜4 個、ビルドが数秒、月額がほぼゼロ、コードのほとんどを
AI が書くことだ。作るのも保守するのも、桁違いに軽い。このサイト(aiseed.dev)自身が、
この構成で動いている。

== この組み合わせは、10 年後も動く

React の v15 で書いたコンポーネントは、v18 ではビルドが通らないことがある。Webpack の
設定は、一年ごとに書き直しが要る。

HTML、CSS、JavaScript の基本仕様は、25 年以上にわたって後方互換が保たれている。1999 年の
HTML ファイルは、今のブラウザでも問題なく開く。Markdown も、2004 年の初版からほとんど
変わっていない。20 年後も同じファイルが読める。

____
フレームワークは時代に依存する。Web 標準と Markdown は時代を超える。
____

== 数字で見る

決めるときに効く数字は、依存の数とビルドの時間だ。React + Next.js + TypeScript +
Tailwind で業務 Web を作ると、依存パッケージは約 1,200 個、ビルドは 3 分、
`node_modules` は 500 MB。同じ機能を HTML + CSS + FastAPI + Markdown で作ると、
依存 4 個、ビルド 5 秒、合計 10 MB になる。

このサイト(aiseed.dev)の規模は、150 ページ以上、合計 30,000 行のコード、24 時間で
組み上げた。React・Next.js・TypeScript はゼロ。Markdown と Mermaid と最小限の HTML/CSS
だけだ。

== 決めたことを、そのまま渡す

この章の決定を一覧にしておく。AI に作らせるときは、これを渡せば足りる。書き方は AI が
知っているので、人が渡すのは決めたことのほうだ。

|===
| 決める所 | 決めたこと
| 中身の形式 | Markdown + Mermaid。中身に HTML タグは書かない
| 外枠 | HTML テンプレート 1〜数ファイル、CSS 1 ファイル、JavaScript は数十行
| 繋ぐ所 | Python。`markdown-it-py` + `Jinja2`(画像が要るときだけ `Pillow`)
| 置き場 | 中身は `articles/`、外枠は `tools/templates/`、出力は `html/`。すべて Git の中に置く
| 置かない物 | JS フレームワーク、ビルドツール、TypeScript、CSS フレームワーク、npm と `node_modules`
| 動く部分 | FastAPI。ORM は積まず SQL を直接書く。テンプレートは Jinja2、返すのは HTML
| 部分更新 | 要るときだけ HTMX。SPA にはしない
| 公開 | 焼いた静的ファイルを配る(手順は 2-11)
|===

== 確かめ方

この章は、次の五つができていれば済みだ。

. 記事一本が Markdown ファイルとして置いてあり、その中に HTML タグが一つも入っていない
. 一つのコマンドでビルドが走り、`html/` の下にページが出て、数秒で終わる
. できた HTML をブラウザで開くと、外枠(ヘッダー・ナビ・フッター)と本文が出て、Mermaid の図が描かれている
. 依存パッケージの一覧が数行に収まり、`node_modules` がどこにも無い
. 同じ Markdown から PDF を作ってみて、中身がそのまま出る

```bash
uv add markdown-it-py jinja2 pillow
python -c "import markdown_it, jinja2; print('ok')"
```

== 人が持つ物

*人が渡す値*

* サイトの構成 ── どのページを出し、どういう順で並べるか
* 配色、ロゴ、書体
* URL の付け方(既存サイトから移すときは、元の URL 構造)
* 公開してよい文章と画像の範囲
* 依存に入れてよいパッケージ

*AI が「やる前に言う」操作*

* 生成先のフォルダを一括で消す、または既存のサイトを上書きする
* 既存の WordPress を停止する、ホスティング契約を解く
* 公開済みの URL 構造を変える
* 自分以外のサイトへクロールを掛ける(`tools/mirror_site.py` の向け先)
* 新しい依存パッケージを足す

== 確かめた版と日付

* `markdown-it-py`、`Jinja2`、`Pillow` ── 版は指定していない
* Playwright、`mermaid-cli` ── 版は指定していない
* FastAPI、asyncpg / psycopg、HTMX ── 版は指定していない
* 手順を書いたのは 2026-09-21
* 版が上がっていたら、AI に公式の手順を確かめさせてから進める

== まとめ

Web を作る道具を、二層に分ける。

* *二層* ── 中身は Markdown と Mermaid、外枠は HTML と CSS と最小限の JavaScript、両者を Python が繋ぐ
* *WordPress から抜ける* ── 七つの手順で Markdown に移す。時間が無ければ Playwright で丸ごと静的に写し取る
* *中身の出口* ── 同じ Markdown が、Web にも PDF にも印刷にも AI への入力にも電子書籍にもなる
* *依存を持たない* ── npm のサプライチェーンの事件は、ほぼ毎年ある。3 個の依存なら全部読み通せる。1,000 個の依存に AI を入れることは、信頼できない他人を 1,000 人招くのと同じだ
* *動く部分* ── FastAPI を一つ選び、ORM も層も積まない
* *時間に強い* ── 25 年後方互換の Web 標準と、20 年変わらない Markdown

ここまでで、公開できる HTML が手元にできた。次章では、その `html/` を自分の側から公開
する。動的な WordPress をやめ、焼いた HTML を Cloudflare Pages に置く ── サーバは
持たない。

'''

== 関連記事

* link:/ai-native-software/web/[2-11: Web を公開する ── Cloudflare Pages]
* link:/ai-native-software/documents/[2-07: 文書を取り戻す ── 原稿は adoc、様式は aiseed office]
* link:/ai-native-software/fastapi/[2-12: API を作る ── FastAPI で基幹のロジックを出す]
* link:/ai-native-software/design/[2-13: 図と資料を作る ── Mermaid・Marp・そのほかの道具]
* link:/ai-native-software/lockin/[3-05: ロックイン問題]
endif::[]
ifdef::lang-en[]
= Build the Web — Back to HTML, CSS, and JavaScript

*Build a website in two layers.*

link:/en/ai-native-software/meetings/[2-09: Meetings and Booking on Your Own Side — Jitsi and Cal.com]
put gatherings and booking on your own side. This chapter assembles, in your own
hands, the web you show to the outside. Content in Markdown and Mermaid, the frame
in HTML, CSS, and minimal JavaScript, with Python connecting the two. Where the
baked HTML then goes is the next chapter's job.

== React fatigue comes from the number of tools

For the past ten years, web development has been an arms race of frameworks.

jQuery, Backbone, Angular, React, Vue, Svelte. Build tools: Grunt, Gulp, Webpack,
Rollup, Vite, Turbopack. CSS: Sass, Less, PostCSS, Tailwind, CSS-in-JS.
Server-side rendering: Next.js, Nuxt, Remix, SvelteKit, Astro.

Each of them solved some problem. But line up the problems they solved against the
problems they created, and it stops being worth it more and more often.

* Build configuration takes weeks to learn
* Dependencies pass 1,000 packages
* Security updates arrive every week
* Builds stop working after a year
* One simple site needs 100 MB of folders

This is not the complexity of the technology. It is complexity we added ourselves.
Come back to raw HTML, CSS, and JavaScript and all five clear at once. The browser
reads those three directly, so no conversion layer sits in between. And those three
plus Markdown are exactly what AI writes most reliably.

== WordPress has four traps

"I'm not using React — we're on WordPress." Many people are thinking exactly that.

That matches the facts. About 43% of the world's websites run on WordPress. In
Japan too — corporate sites, personal blogs, news media, government sites — it is
everywhere.

WordPress has its own traps, though. They differ in nature from the React traps,
and are at least as serious, often more.

*Trap 1: the content sits in MySQL*

WordPress posts are not text files. They are records inside MySQL, mixed with HTML.
Convert the same post to PDF, move it to another site, hand it to AI for analysis —
every path starts with an export.

And the export format is `.xml` (WordPress's own WXR), with HTML tags and
proprietary shortcodes (like `[gallery]`) mixed in. The content stays on the
WordPress side. That is the same shape as the "data still inside Excel" seen in
link:/en/ai-native-software/foundation/[2-03: Lay the Foundation — SQLite, PostgreSQL, pgvector, DuckDB, Polars].

*Trap 2: plugins widen the attack surface*

WordPress extends through plugins built by volunteers around the world. Twenty to
thirty plugins on one site is ordinary. Each surfaces vulnerabilities periodically.
The whole site becomes a collection of attack surfaces. That 43% of the world's web
runs on the same machinery means, from the attacker's side, that 43% of the targets
are the same machinery.

*Trap 3: updates stop each other*

WordPress core, themes, plugins — all of them depend on each other. Update one and
another stops working. "We applied an update and the site went down" is a common
event in WordPress operations.

*Trap 4: Python gives steadier AI output than PHP*

WordPress themes and plugins are written in PHP. Claude can write PHP, but output
is steadier in Python. If you are building tools alongside AI, leaning to the Python
side is faster.

The urgent case is the online shop. It handles payments and customer personal data,
which makes it a high-value target — card skimming, data leaks. The damage lands
directly on money and trust. A WordPress shop should go static first of all. Hand
payment to an external checkout page such as Stripe and never hold card data
yourself. Let go of the running PHP, the database, and the admin login, and the
attack surface goes with them.

== Escaping WordPress takes seven steps

If you are on WordPress, the way out is settled. You move in the same *parallel
operation* shape covered in
link:/en/ai-native-software/fastapi/[2-12: Build an API — Expose Core Logic with FastAPI].

. Keep the existing WordPress running, and export the content to Markdown (plugins or CLI tools automate it; Claude writes the converter)
. Build the new site with Markdown + minimal HTML/CSS + Python
. Preserve the URL structure (to carry the search equity over)
. Verify behavior in a staging environment and prepare the reindex requests
. Pick the DNS cutover date
. After cutover, run WordPress read-only for one month
. If nothing surfaces, stop WordPress and cancel the hosting contract

The cost difference is large too. WordPress managed hosting (WP Engine, Kinsta and
the like) runs tens to hundreds of dollars a month. Put static HTML on Cloudflare
Pages or GitHub Pages and the monthly cost is zero. Tens of thousands of yen a year
come back.

____
WordPress, too, is left through parallel operation. Export to Markdown, write only
the frame in HTML and CSS, generate with Python, serve it static. That is how you
stop WordPress.
____

If there is no time to rebuild, there is a faster move — *mirror the existing site
whole into static files*. A headless browser (Playwright) opens each page, saves the
HTML and every file it loads (CSS, JS, images, fonts), and rewrites references to
relative paths. By default it does not execute JavaScript; it saves the raw HTML the
server returns — light HTML, free of the huge DOM that ad and analytics widgets add
at render time. Turn rendering on only for sites that assemble their body with JS.
Either way the browser follows and downloads every file the page actually pulls in,
so nothing slips through the way it does with `requests` or `wget`. Put that on
static hosting and the running WordPress stops, attack surface and all. What
SiteSucker (Mac, paid) does, AI can write for you in Python (this site's repository
ships `tools/mirror_site.py`).

== Split content from frame, in two layers

What is in a website splits into two.

|===
| Kind | What you write | Tool
| Content | Prose, tables, quotes, code, diagrams | Markdown + Mermaid
| Frame | Header, navigation, footer, layout, color | HTML + CSS + minimal JavaScript
| Connection | Pour content into the frame and emit HTML | Python
|===

Writing these mixed together has been the catch in web production so far. A React
component holds prose, formatting, and logic in one place. A WordPress post holds
HTML tags and prose in one place. The mixture makes itself felt later, when
something has to be rewritten.

The AI-native split keeps content and frame fully apart. The content is written in
Markdown and Mermaid only, with no HTML tags. The frame is written in HTML and CSS
and never touches the content of any individual article. Python connects them
mechanically.

```mermaid
flowchart LR
  subgraph Content["content (reusable at other exits)"]
    MD["article *.md<br/>(Markdown)"]
    MMD["diagram *.mmd<br/>(Mermaid)"]
  end
  subgraph Frame["frame"]
    HTML["template.html"]
    CSS["style.css"]
    JS["main.js (minimal)"]
  end
  Py(("Python<br/>build"))

  MD --> Py
  MMD --> Py
  HTML --> Py
  CSS --> Py
  JS --> Py
  Py --> Site["static HTML site"]

  MD -.->|as is| PDF["PDF / print"]
  MD -.->|as is| AI[("AI analysis")]
  MD -.->|as is| Book["e-book"]

  classDef good fill:#e8f5e9,stroke:#7a9a6d,color:#3a4d34
  classDef bad  fill:#fef3e7,stroke:#c89559,color:#5a3f1a
  class MD,MMD good
  class HTML,CSS,JS bad
```

== Holding content in Markdown adds exits

The biggest reason to write content in Markdown and Mermaid is that the same data
travels to exits beyond the web.

From the same Markdown file:

* A web page (Python converts it to HTML)
* A PDF (`pandoc` or Claude does the conversion)
* A print-ready manuscript
* An e-book (EPUB)
* Input to AI for summary, translation, and questions
* Material to paste into other media
* Diffs and history in Git
* Co-editing with other people

The content does not stay on the web side. Content written in WordPress or Wix ends
when the service ends. Content in Notion lives inside Notion's format. Markdown
belongs nowhere in particular.

This is the web version of the "hold the content as plain text" idea from
link:/en/ai-native-software/documents/[2-07: Take Documents Back — Manuscripts in AsciiDoc, Forms in aiseed office].
Separate entrance, content, and exit.

* Entrance — Claude turns images, PDFs, audio, and Word into text
* Content — held in Markdown and Mermaid, versioned in Git
* Exit — web, PDF, print, AI analysis, e-book. Python converts per use

== Write the frame minimally

The frame — header, navigation, footer, layout, color — is written directly in HTML
and CSS. Minimal is enough.

* HTML templates: one to a few files
* CSS: one file (a few hundred to a few thousand lines)
* JavaScript: only where it is genuinely needed (a mobile menu toggle, say), tens of lines

You touch these a few times a year. Do not spend too much on the frame. The skeleton
stays a skeleton, and the time goes into the content.

*What you keep in hand*

* HTML (structure) — one template file
* CSS (appearance) — one file
* JavaScript (only the moving parts) — tens of lines
* Markdown + Mermaid (content) — one file per article

*What you stop needing*

* JavaScript frameworks (React, Vue, Angular, Svelte)
* Build tools (Webpack, Vite, Turbopack, Parcel)
* TypeScript (plain JS is enough)
* CSS frameworks (Tailwind, Bootstrap)
* Package managers (npm, yarn, pnpm) and `node_modules`

What remains is raw web standards and Markdown. HTML / CSS / JS have no conversion
layer, and Markdown is the notation AI handles best. Both can be placed exactly as
Claude returns them.

== The other reason to leave npm is the supply chain

Slow builds and heavy dependencies are not the whole of it. The other reason to
leave npm is the structure itself: *other people's code runs on your machine,
through the registry*.

Supply-chain attacks on the npm registry happen repeatedly. Typosquatting that lures
a mistaken install with a similar package name, code injected through a dependency,
package takeover through a compromised maintainer account, secrets exfiltrated in a
postinstall script. The verifiable landmarks alone line up like this.

* 2018: the `event-stream` incident (aimed at cryptocurrency wallets)
* 2021: the `ua-parser-js` incident (mining and info-stealing malware injected)
* 2022: the `colors` and `faker` incidents (destructive commits by the author)
* 2024: the `xz-utils` backdoor (not npm, but the classic case of a long-running maintainer takeover)

Nearly every year, a large incident is reported. npm packages distributed officially
by major companies have repeatedly become stepping stones for attacks through the
supply chain. For any individual incident, go to the primary sources yourself with
the procedure in
link:/en/ai-native-software/verify-narratives/[3-02: Checking the Story — Verifying Vendor Narratives Against Primary Sources].

=== The structure comes from npm's design

The issue lies in the design of the npm ecosystem itself.

* One project depends transitively on hundreds to thousands of packages
* Those packages are maintained by individuals all over the world
* Updates arrive automatically (`npm install` pulls the latest)
* The package manager executes dependency code without inspecting it
* Arbitrary code runs in build steps and postinstall hooks

Take over any one of them and it reaches every project that pulled it in. One
package affecting 1,000 repositories transitively is a shape that comes along with
npm. How that shape plays out over time meshes with
link:/en/ai-native-software/lockin/[3-05: The Lock-In Problem].

Against that, this chapter's stack carries far fewer dependencies. Markdown + HTML +
CSS + JS has zero external dependencies (browser standards only), and the Python
build script has three: `markdown-it-py`, `Jinja2`, `Pillow`. With three, reading
all of the source together with Claude is within reach, and odd behavior is
noticeable. A tree of 1,000 is beyond what a person's eyes can follow. Python
packages are scrutinized more heavily the more important they are on PyPI, and
arbitrary code running at install time is not the norm there. The surface that can
be compromised is smaller by orders of magnitude.

=== What it means to put AI into 1,000 dependencies

There is one more weight to this in the age of AI.

Claude Code reads files and runs commands behind the editor. If a malicious package
is sitting somewhere in `node_modules` on the development machine, *its execution
can be triggered through the AI*.

____
Putting AI into a project that carries 1,000 dependencies is the same as inviting
1,000 strangers you cannot vouch for into your own workspace.
____

A stack with no dependencies carries none of that worry. HTML, CSS, and Markdown are
just text. The only code that runs is code you and Claude wrote. Not merely "light"
and "fast," but "no entrance to be compromised" — that is the other reason to hold
dependencies down.

== Python connects content and frame

Turning Markdown into HTML, Mermaid diagrams into SVG or images, and pouring them
into the frame template — that is the job of a Python script.

```
articles/foo/ja.md  ──→  Python  ──→  html/foo/index.html
                              ↑
                  tools/templates/article.html (frame)
```

You do not need to write that script yourself. Ask Claude to write it. Convert
Markdown to HTML with `markdown-it-py`, pour it into the template with `Jinja2`.
Together that is 100–200 lines.

Three or four dependencies cover it.

* `markdown-it-py` — a CommonMark parser
* `Jinja2` — an HTML template engine
* `Pillow` — image processing (OG image generation, only when needed)
* Mermaid diagrams render in the browser, or go to SVG with `mermaid-cli`

Type `uv add markdown-it-py jinja2 pillow` once, and from there `git clone && uv
sync` brings it up on another machine. No `node_modules`, and no `requirements.txt`
either.

This site's build script (`tools/build_article.py`) was mostly written by Claude.
The build tool can be held in your own hands now. Instead of following a framework's
conventions, write under your own. Diagrams and documents continue in
link:/en/ai-native-software/design/[2-13: Make Diagrams and Documents — Mermaid, Marp, and the Other Tools].

== For the moving parts, pick one FastAPI

"You can't build dynamic sites that way," you might think. The moving parts go on
the server side. Python's FastAPI, that one alone.

Flask, Django, Go, Rust, Ruby — there are mountains of choices. But each added
choice divides the organization by that much. "We write in FastAPI" — once decided,
the argument past that point ends. *Pick, then forget you picked.* That is how tool
choice works here.

Why FastAPI:

* It is Python, so it lines up with the rest of this series' stack
* Type hints and Pydantic work naturally
* async is standard
* OpenAPI documentation generates itself
* Claude writes it best (open source, with rich training data)
* Little boilerplate

And that FastAPI is kept minimal too.

* No ORM. Write SQL directly with a PostgreSQL driver (asyncpg or psycopg)
* No stacked layers. No repository layer, service layer, or domain layer — receive the request, run the SQL, return the HTML
* No growing dependencies. FastAPI, the PostgreSQL driver, and Jinja2 (when needed) are enough
* No growing config files. Environment variables run it
* One process

One handler takes this shape.

```python
@app.get("/items", response_class=HTMLResponse)
async def items():
    rows = await conn.fetch("SELECT name, price FROM items")
    return render("items.html", rows=rows)
```

Receive the request, run the SQL, return the HTML. From there, keep asking what more
is actually needed. Since Claude writes SQL directly, it reaches without an ORM's
abstraction in between. The AI already knows the syntax; what the human hands over
is the decisions.

Client-side JavaScript stays minimal as well. `<a>` handles link navigation,
`<form>` handles submission. If partial updates are needed, HTMX (a few KB library,
not a framework). Add WebSocket when it becomes genuinely necessary. Do not start
with an SPA.

How to write FastAPI, and how to expose core logic as an API, belong to
link:/en/ai-native-software/fastapi/[2-12: Build an API — Expose Core Logic with FastAPI].
Here, the reason for the choice is enough.

== What this shape builds

"Content in Markdown, frame in HTML and CSS, Python in between" reads faster in
cases.

* *A sole proprietor's portfolio* — one `.md` per work plus images, one template file and one `style.css`, a 50-line Python build. 30–100 pages, build in seconds, zero per month
* *A small business's corporate site* — about, services, contact, and blog as Markdown. The contact form is 50 lines of FastAPI over SMTP; the job list is 30 lines of SQLite + FastAPI. 50–200 pages, under $10 a month
* *A non-profit's event announcements* — one Markdown file per event, like `events/2026-04-foo.md`. Python generates the yearly index; sign-ups go to an external form link or 30 lines of FastAPI
* *A school's class newsletter* (parents only) — weekly Markdown and photos, one `style.css` in the school colors. Basic auth (`htpasswd`), or behind the gate from link:/en/ai-native-software/auth/[2-05: Stand Up the Gate — One Login with PocketBase]
* *A research group's papers and data* — the paper manuscripts plus dataset metadata, with the data downloaded directly as Parquet

What they share: three or four dependency packages, builds in seconds, nearly zero
monthly cost, and AI writing most of the code. Both building and maintaining are
lighter by orders of magnitude. This site (aiseed.dev) itself runs in this shape.

== This combination still runs in ten years

A component written for React v15 may not build under v18. Webpack configuration
needs rewriting every year.

The core specs of HTML, CSS, and JavaScript have held backward compatibility for
25-plus years. An HTML file from 1999 opens fine in today's browser. Markdown too has
barely changed since its 2004 first edition. The same files will read twenty years
from now.

____
Frameworks depend on their era. Web standards and Markdown cross eras.
____

== In numbers

The numbers that matter when deciding are the dependency count and the build time.
Building a business web with React + Next.js + TypeScript + Tailwind: around 1,200
dependency packages, 3-minute builds, 500 MB of `node_modules`. The same
functionality with HTML + CSS + FastAPI + Markdown: 4 dependencies, 5-second builds,
10 MB in total.

The scale of this site (aiseed.dev): 150-plus pages, 30,000 lines of code in total,
assembled in 24 hours. Zero React, Next.js, or TypeScript. Markdown, Mermaid, and
minimal HTML/CSS only.

== Hand the decisions over as they are

Here are this chapter's decisions in one place. When you have AI build the site,
handing this over is enough. The AI already knows the syntax; what the human hands
over is the decisions.

|===
| Decision point | What is decided
| Content format | Markdown + Mermaid. No HTML tags inside the content
| Frame | One to a few HTML templates, one CSS file, tens of lines of JavaScript
| The connection | Python. `markdown-it-py` + `Jinja2` (`Pillow` only when images are needed)
| Where things live | Content in `articles/`, frame in `tools/templates/`, output in `html/` — all inside Git
| What is not placed | JS frameworks, build tools, TypeScript, CSS frameworks, npm and `node_modules`
| Moving parts | FastAPI. No ORM; SQL written directly. Jinja2 for templates, HTML as the response
| Partial updates | HTMX only when needed. No SPA
| Publishing | Serve the baked static files (the procedure is 2-11)
|===

== How to check you are done

This chapter is done when these five hold.

. One article sits there as a Markdown file, with no HTML tag inside it
. One command runs the build, pages appear under `html/`, and it finishes in seconds
. Opening the produced HTML in a browser shows the frame (header, navigation, footer) and the body, with the Mermaid diagram drawn
. The dependency list fits in a few lines, and `node_modules` is nowhere
. Making a PDF from the same Markdown gives you the content as it is

```bash
uv add markdown-it-py jinja2 pillow
python -c "import markdown_it, jinja2; print('ok')"
```

== What the human holds

*Values the human supplies*

* The site's structure — which pages exist, and in what order
* Colors, logo, typeface
* How URLs are formed (when moving from an existing site, its URL structure)
* The range of prose and images that may be published
* Which packages may enter the dependencies

*Actions the AI states before performing*

* Deleting the output folder wholesale, or overwriting an existing site
* Stopping the existing WordPress, or cancelling the hosting contract
* Changing a published URL structure
* Pointing a crawl at a site that is not yours (where `tools/mirror_site.py` is aimed)
* Adding a new dependency package

== Versions checked, and when

* `markdown-it-py`, `Jinja2`, `Pillow` — no version pinned
* Playwright, `mermaid-cli` — no version pinned
* FastAPI, asyncpg / psycopg, HTMX — no version pinned
* The procedure was written on 2026-09-21
* If a version has moved, have the AI confirm the official procedure before proceeding

== Summary

Split the web-building tools into two layers.

* *Two layers* — content in Markdown and Mermaid, the frame in HTML, CSS, and minimal JavaScript, with Python connecting them
* *Leaving WordPress* — seven steps move the content to Markdown. With no time to rebuild, Playwright mirrors the whole site into static files
* *Exits for the content* — the same Markdown becomes the web, a PDF, print, input to AI, and an e-book
* *Holding no dependencies* — npm supply-chain incidents land nearly every year. Three dependencies can all be read through. Putting AI into 1,000 of them invites 1,000 strangers in
* *The moving parts* — pick one FastAPI, with no ORM and no stacked layers
* *Durable in time* — web standards with 25 years of backward compatibility, and Markdown unchanged for 20

At this point the HTML that can be published exists in your hands. The next chapter
publishes that `html/` from your own side. Dynamic WordPress goes, the baked HTML
goes onto Cloudflare Pages — and you hold no server.

'''

== Related articles

* link:/en/ai-native-software/web/[2-11: Publish the Web — Cloudflare Pages (a WordPress Replacement)]
* link:/en/ai-native-software/documents/[2-07: Take Documents Back — Manuscripts in AsciiDoc, Forms in aiseed office]
* link:/en/ai-native-software/fastapi/[2-12: Build an API — Expose Core Logic with FastAPI]
* link:/en/ai-native-software/design/[2-13: Make Diagrams and Documents — Mermaid, Marp, and the Other Tools]
* link:/en/ai-native-software/lockin/[3-05: The Lock-In Problem]
endif::[]

title.en: Publish the Web — Your Own Machine, or Cloudflare Pages

subtitle.en: Ship the baked HTML to either home, by the same procedure
description.ja: 前章で焼いた静的サイトを、外に出す。置き場は二つある。2-02 で立てた自分の一台に Caddy で載せるか、Cloudflare Pages に置くか。どちらも「ビルド → 確認 → デプロイ」を分ける同じ手順で出せて、後から入れ替えられる。自分の一台なら、公開の Web も内部の道具も動く部分も同じ機械に並び、証明書は Caddy が自動で取る。Cloudflare Pages なら、CDN と防御を借りて機械の面倒を持たない。動く部分は FastAPI 一つにまとめ、公開の側からはその一点だけを通す。人が持つのは、ドメインと鍵と、本番へ出す判断の三つだ。
description.en: Ship the static site you baked in the previous chapter. There are two homes for it: your own machine from 2-02 with Caddy in front, or Cloudflare Pages. Both take the same procedure — build, verify, deploy as separate steps — and either can replace the other later. On your own machine, the public web, the internal tools, and the moving parts all sit on one box, and Caddy takes the certificate automatically. On Cloudflare Pages, you borrow the CDN and the defense and hold no machine. The moving parts are one FastAPI, and the public side reaches exactly that one point. The human holds the domain, the keys, and the decision to go live.

label: Independence 11
